arXiv:2406.02424v2  [cs.LG]  3 Apr 2025Contextual Dynamic Pricing: Algorithms, Optimality,
and Local Diﬀerential Privacy Constraints
Zifeng Zhao1, Feiyu Jiang2, Yi Yu3∗
Abstract
We study contextual dynamic pricing problems where a ﬁrm sells prod ucts toTsequentially-arriving
consumers, behaving according to an unknown demand model. The ﬁ rm aims to minimize its regret over
a clairvoyant that knows the model in advance. The demand follows a generalized linear model (GLM),
allowing for stochastic feature vectors in Rdencoding product and consumer information. We ﬁrst show
the optimal regret is of order√
dT, up to logarithmic factors, improving existing upper bounds by a√
d
factor. This optimal rate is materialized by two algorithms: a conﬁde nce bound-type algorithm and an
explore-then-commit (ETC) algorithm. A key insight is an intrinsic con nection between dynamic pricing
and contextual multi-armed bandit problems with many arms with a ca reful discretization.
We further study contextual dynamic pricing under local diﬀerent ial privacy (LDP) constraints. We
propose a stochastic gradient descent-based ETC algorithm achie ving regret upper bounds of order
d√
T/ǫ, up to logarithmic factors, where ǫ >0 is the privacy parameter. The upper bounds with and
without LDP constraints are matched by newly constructed minimax lower bounds, characterizing costs
ofprivacy. Moreover,weextendourstudytodynamicpricingundermixedpriva cyconstraints, improving
the privacy-utility tradeoﬀ by leveraging public data. This is the ﬁrst time such setting is studied in
the dynamic pricing literature and our theoretical results seamless ly bridge dynamic pricing with and
without LDP. Extensive numerical experiments and real data applications are co nducted to illustrate the
eﬃciency and practical value of our algorithms.
Keywords : Onlinelearning,Conﬁdencebound, Explore-then-commit,Diﬀeren tialprivacy,Minimaxoptimality.
∗1. University of Notre Dame; 2. Fudan University; 3. Univers ity of Warwick

1 Introduction
With the technological advances and prevalence of online ma rketplaces, many ﬁrms can now dynam-
ically make pricing decisions while having access to an abun dance of contextual information such as
consumer characteristics, product features and the econom ic environment. On the other hand, in
practice, the demand model is unknown in advance and ﬁrms nee d to dynamically learn how the
contextual information impacts consumer demand. Thus, to m aximize its revenue, the ﬁrm needs
to implement dynamic pricing, which aims to optimally balan ce the trade-oﬀ between learning the
unknown demand function and earning revenues by exploiting the estimated demand model. Due to
its importance in revenue management, dynamic pricing has b een extensively studied across the ﬁelds
of statistics, machine learning, and operations research u nder various settings. We refer the readers
to Section S.1for recent works and den Boer (2015) for a comprehensive revi ew.
In this work, we consider the problem of contextual dynamic p ricing, where the ﬁrm sells products
toT∈N+sequentially arriving consumers and the unknown demand fol lows a generalized linear
model (GLM). In particular, at each time period t∈ {1,...,T}, the seller is given a d-dimensional
feature vector zt∈Rd, which encodes the contextual information, and needs to set a priceptin a
compact price interval [ l,u].Given (zt,pt), the demand ytfollows a GLM with an unknown parameter
θ= (α,β) such that E(yt|zt,pt;θ) =m/parenleftbig
α⊤zt−(β⊤zt)pt/parenrightbig
, wherem(·) is the inverse of the GLM link
function. For example, we have m(x) =xfor linear regression and m(x) = 1/{1+exp(−x)}for logistic
regression. Here, the α⊤ztterm models the intrinsic utility of the product and β⊤ztcaptures the price
elasticity. The revenue of the ﬁrm is deﬁned as rt=ptyt.Due to its interpretability and ﬂexibility,
this GLM demand model including the linear special case (i.e .m(x) =x) has been widely studied in
the dynamic pricing literature under various scenarios (e. g. Ban and Keskin, 2021; Wang et al., 2024;
Bastani et al., 2022; Chen et al., 2022c; Lei et al., 2023).
In the vanilla version of this problem, the ﬁrm aims to design a policy that utilizes the raw
context{zt}and maximizes its expected revenue E/parenleftbig/summationtextT
t=1rt/parenrightbig
over theTperiods (i.e. minimizes its
regret over a clairvoyant that knows θin advance). In this paper, we ﬁrst propose two algorithms an d
2
show that they achieve a near-optimal regret of order /tildewideO(√
dT) underthis setting, an improvement over
the existing /tildewideO(d√
T) regret upper bound in the literature (e.g. Ban and Keskin, 2 021; Wang et al.,
2024). In other words, the regret is sublinear in the dimension of the context vector.
To build intuition, consider the special (and simpliﬁed) ca se of a linear demand model with yt=
α⊤zt−(β⊤zt)pt+εt, where{εt}T
t=1is an i.i.d. mean zero error process. Denote e(i) as theith standard
basis of Rdfori∈ {1,...,d}. We further set the context vector zt=e(⌈td/T⌉), assuming Tis a
multiplier of d. Under this simpliﬁed setting, we are essentially dealing w ithdindependent dynamic
pricing problems. In particular, the ith sub-problem runs for time t∈ {(i−1)T/d+1,···,iT/d}with
a total ofT/dperiods and assumes a linear demand model with nocontext (i.e. context-free). That is
yt=αi−βipt+εt, whereαiandβidenote theith entry ofαandβ.The optimal regret of this context-
free sub-problem, due to Broder and Rusmevichientong (2012 ), is of order /tildewideO(/radicalbig
T/d). Summing up
over alldsub-problems, intuitively, we can have a regret upper bound of order /tildewideO(d/radicalbig
T/d) =/tildewideO(√
dT).
In recent years, there has been a growing concern over data pr ivacy (Lucas et al., 2021), as con-
sumers become increasingly aware of the data collected abou t them and their use in personalized
pricing and recommendation. Based on diﬀerential privacy (e.g. Dwork et al., 2006), industry-led ini-
tiatives have emerged to address these concerns, such as the RAPPOR project (Google, 2014) and the
integration of privacy safeguards into Siri (Apple, 2019). In this paper, we further study contextual
dynamic pricing under the local diﬀerential privacy (LDP) co nstraint (e.g. Evﬁmievski et al., 2003;
Duchi et al., 2018). LDP provides a decentralized version of diﬀerential privacy in the sense that it
ensures sensitive personal information remains protected even if the data security of the focal ﬁrm is
compromised. For ǫ>0, anǫ-LDP algorithm masks the sensitive context ztat any time tby ensuring
that for any two possible outcomes of the raw data, the probab ilities of their privatized data lying in
any given measurable set diﬀer by no more than a multiplicativ e constant exp( ǫ). Therefore, a smaller
ǫimplies higher privacy. Leveraging stochastic gradient de scent (SGD), we devise the ﬁrst dynamic
pricing policy in the literature that is ǫ-LDP and achieves a near-optimal regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T/ǫ)
under the GLM demand model, therefore characterizing the co st of privacy in parametric contextual
3
dynamic pricing.
1.1 Our contributions
First, we thoroughly study the contextual dynamic pricing p roblem with a parametric GLM demand
function and characterize its near-optimal regret upper bo und. In particular, we propose two pricing
policies in Section 2and Section S.3, an ETC algorithm and a conﬁdence bound-type (supCB) algo-
rithm, and show that both achieve a regret of order /tildewideO(√
dT), withdandTbeing the dimension of
feature vectors and total number of consumers. Thus, we impr ove the existing upper bound /tildewideO(d√
T)
in the literature (e.g. Ban and Keskin, 2021; Wang et al., 202 4; Simchi-Levi and Wang, 2023) by a
factor of√
d, suggesting the impact of dimension dto regret can be controlled at a sublinear rate.
Under the condition that the optimal price is unique and an in terior point of [ l,u], a common as-
sumptionin thedynamicpricingliterature, weshow in Secti on2.2that atuning-freeand computation-
ally eﬃcient ETC algorithm can achieve the optimal regret up to a logarithmic term. This is diﬀerent
from the contextual multi-armed bandit (MAB) literature wh ere ETC is in general sub-optimal. The
key insight is that dueto the structure of the reward functio n (i.e. the expected revenue), the regret in-
curredduetoestimation error(ormoreprecisely, predicti onerror)scales withthemagnitudeof /bar⌈bl/hatwideθ−θ/bar⌈bl2
insteadof /bar⌈bl/hatwideθ−θ/bar⌈bl. WerefertoProposition S.5.2anditsdiscussion(presentedfollowingTheorem 2.2)for
more details. We further examine the cause of sub-optimalit y of the existing upperboundbased on the
popular MLE-Cycle algorithm (e.g. Broder and Rusmevichien tong, 2012; Ban and Keskin, 2021) and
Semi-Myopicalgorithm(e.g.Keskin and Zeevi,2014;Den Boe r and Zwart,2014;Simchi-Levi and Wang,
2023) for contextual dynamic pricing, which is under-explo ration with respect to dimension d, and
suggest a simple ﬁx to make it optimal.
ThesupCBalgorithm proposedinSection S.3isbuiltuponaconﬁdenceboundtypealgorithm. The
key ingredient of supCB is a novel discovery of an intrinsic c onnection between contextual dynamic
pricing and contextual MAB. We show that a carefully designe d discretization of the continuous
action space [ l,u] can transform the dynamic pricing problem to an MAB problem with negligible
approximation error. Importantly, this connection furthe r helps supCB achieve the near-optimal
4
/tildewideO(√
dT) regret upper bound under weaker conditions than those impo sed in the existing literature.
Second, we consider in Section 3the important and timely setting where the consumer data are
subject to LDP constraints. Building upon Duchi et al. (2018 ) and Chen et al. (2022a), we extend the
notion of LDP to the setting of parametric contextual dynami c pricing. Based on SGD in conjunction
with theL2-ball mechanism in Duchi et al. (2018), we design an eﬃcient E TC-type algorithm that is
provablyǫ-LDP and achieves a near-optimal regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T/ǫ). To our best knowledge, this is
the ﬁrst LDP algorithm for contextual dynamic pricing under the GLM demand model and provides
theﬁrstcharacterization ofthecost of LDPin thetask ofpar ametric dynamicpricing. As abyproduct,
we study the SGD estimator under ǫ-LDP and carefully analyze the bias-variance trade-oﬀ due t o an
additional truncation step in the L2-ball mechanism for handling unbounded gradients. We further
show that our policy can be readily modiﬁed to cover dynamic p ricing under approximate ( ǫ,∆)-LDP
constraints, a less stringent notion than the ǫ-LDP. Moreover, we study dynamic pricing under mixed
privacy constraints in Section 3.3, where consumers exhibi t heterogeneous privacy preferences and
the ﬁrm can access raw information from the non-private cons umers. We design an ETC-LDP-Mixed
algorithm that eﬃciently combines a private and a non-priva te SGD to further improve the privacy-
utility tradeoﬀ by leveraging the public data. We show its re gret upper bound seamlessly bridges the
/tildewideO(√
dT) bound in non-private pricing and the /tildewideO(d√
T/ǫ) bound in ǫ-LDP dynamic pricing.
Third, to demonstrate the optimality of our proposed method s with and without LDP constraints,
we further establish the accompanying minimax lower bounds in Section 4. The proofs of lower
bounds are based on a novel construction of 2dindistinguishable demand models. The results, i.e. the
Ω(√
dT) lower bound under the non-private setting and the Ω( d√
T/ǫ) lower bound under the LDP
setting, are ﬁrst time seen in the literature. We note that co mpared to lower bound constructions
for statistical estimation under the LDP setting (e.g. Duch i et al., 2018; Acharya et al., 2024), where
data are independent, our lower bound construction is much m ore involved as data are adaptively
collected due to the pricing policy. Extensive numerical re sults including both simulations and real
data applications are conducted in Section 5to support our theoretical ﬁndings and illustrate the
5
eﬃciency of the proposed algorithms over existing ones in th e contextual dynamic pricing literature.
Literature Review : Three streams of literature are closely related to our pape r: dynamic pricing
with demand learning, contextual MAB, and diﬀerential priva cy for online learning. We provide an
in-depth literature review on related works in Section S.1 o f the supplement.
The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we formally introduce the problem setup
and propose the supCB and ETC algorithms. In Section 3, we further consider contextual dynamic
pricing under the additional LDP constraint. Minimax lower bounds are derived in Section 4. We
conclude with extensive numerical studies in Section 5.

v⊤M−1v. For
any positive integer K, denote [K] ={1,...,K}. For any vector vand setS, let ΠS(v) be theL2-
projection of vontoS. We letO(·),o(·), Θ(·) and Ω( ·) denote Landau’s Big-O, little-o, Big-Theta
and Big-Omega, respectively. The notation /tildewideO(·) disregards poly-logarithmic factors for O(·).
2 An improved upper bound on the regret
Inthis section, we providean improved upperboundon thereg ret forcontextual dynamicpricing. The
detailed problem setup is introduced in Section 2.1, and two algorithms, namely supCB and ETC, are
proposed and analyzed in Section 2.2.Section 2.2.2 further discusses the case of adversarial con texts.
2.1 Problem setup
We consider a ﬁrm, hereafter referred to as the seller, that sells a product to T∈N+sequentially
arriving consumers. In each time period t∈[T], the seller observes contextual information featurized
as a vector zt∈Rd, which may include consumer personal information and produ ct characteristics.
Upon observing zt, the seller oﬀers a price ptfrom a compact price interval [ l,u]⊆R+to the consumer
and observes a demand yt∈R. Note that we do not require the knowledge of T.

2.2 The ETC algorithm
In this section, we propose an explore-then-commit (ETC) al gorithm in Algorithm 1, which is tuning-
free and computationally eﬃcient, and show that it achieves the optimal regret for dynamic pricing.
Algorithm 1 The ETC algorithm for dynamic pricing
Input: Total rounds T, price interval [ l,u], exploration length τ.
a. (Exploration): For t∈[τ], uniformly choose pt∈[l,u], recordytandxt= (z⊤
t,−ptz⊤
t)⊤into the
price experiment set Ψ.
b. (Estimation): Obtain MLE /hatwideθbased on observations in Ψ.
c. (Exploitation): For t=τ+1,...,T, oﬀer the greedy price at /hatwidep∗
t=p∗(/hatwideθ,zt) = argmax
p∈[l,u]r(p,zt,/hatwideθ).
ETC employs a simple two-stage structure: in the exploratio n stage, it conducts τrounds of price
experimentswherepricesarerandomlydrawnfrom[ l,u]; itthenobtainsanMLE /hatwideθbasedonthesample
collected and switches to the exploitation stage, where it u ses/hatwideθto set prices for the rest T−τrounds.
We remark that ETC is a standard algorithm in the bandit liter ature (e.g. Lattimore and Szepesv´ ari,
2020) and is recently used in semi-parametric dynamic prici ng in Fan et al. (2024). Importantly, ETC
only requires a single computation of MLE, which is highly eﬃ cient.

Σp= [1,−µp;−µp,µ2

p]∈R2×2. It is easy to see that µp= (u+l)/2,σ2

Write Σx= Σp⊗Σz, which is the covariance matrix of the feature xt= (z⊤
t,−z⊤

which quantiﬁes the information gain from each consumer dur ing price exploration. We proceed by
introducing Assumption 2.2, which is needed for theoretical guarantees of ETC.
Assumption 2.2. There exists an absolute constant ςo>0 such that for any θ∈ {θ:/bar⌈blθ−θ∗/bar⌈bl ≤ςo}
and any context vector z∈¯Z, with¯Zbeing the closure of Z, it holds that the optimal price p∗=
argmaxp∈[l,u]r(p,z,θ) is unique and is an interior point of the price range [ l,u].
We remark that Assumption 2.2is a mild assumption commonly used in the dynamic pricing lit -
erature under various settings (e.g. Broder and Rusmevichi entong, 2012; Javanmard and Nazerzadeh,
2019; Ban and Keskin, 2021; Chen and Gallego, 2021; Lei et al. , 2023; Fan et al., 2024). In particular,
it requires that the optimal price p∗is unique for all context vector and all θwithin an arbitrarily
small neighborhood of θ∗.To understand Assumption 2.2, consider a linear demand model. We have
that the optimal price is unique over Rand isp∗=z⊤α/(2z⊤β) for anyzandθ= (α⊤,β⊤)⊤.There-
fore, given that the true price elasticity z⊤β∗is lower bounded by an absolute constant cbsuch that
z⊤β∗≥cb>0 for allz∈¯Z(i.e. all consumers are sensitive to prices), one can always ﬁnd a suﬃciently
large interval [ l,u] and suﬃciently small ςosuch that Assumption 2.2holds. By simple algebra, the
same holds under a logistic demand model. In the following, w ithout loss of generality, we assume
ςo∈(0,1].
An important implication of Assumption 2.2is that the optimal price p∗is a Lipschitz continuous
functionofθover/bar⌈blθ−θ∗/bar⌈bl ≤ςouniformlyforall z∈ Z, asstated inProposition S.5.2ofthesupplement.
In other words, when the parameter estimation error /bar⌈bl/hatwideθ−θ∗/bar⌈blis suﬃciently small, its impact on the
estimation error of the optimal price is Lipschitz. Such a Li pschitz continuity property is explicitly

Remark 2.1 (Comparison with MLE-Cycle and Semi-Myopic ).Two popular algorithms in the
dynamic pricing literature are the MLE-Cycle algorithm (e. g. Broder and Rusmevichientong, 2012;
Ban and Keskin,2021) andtheSemi-Myopictypealgorithm(e.g.Keskin and Zeevi,2 014;Simchi-Levi and Wang,
2023),both achieving a regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T). The key idea of MLE-Cycle is to divide the horizon
N+into cycles. The cth cycle,c∈N+, is of length c+k, wherek∈N+is aconstant. Within each
cycle, the ﬁrst krounds are used for price experiments and the next crounds for price exploitation.
The key idea of Semi-Myopic is to run a greedy dynamic pricing policy but further inject a controlled
deviation (in particular, of size κt−1/4for some constantκ >0) into the greedily estimated optimal
price. In Sections S.5.4 and S.5.5 of the supplement, we show the sub-optimality of MLE-Cycle and
Semi-Myopic is due to under-exploration, i.e. the constant kandκdonotscale with the dimension d
optimally. Moreover, we show that via a simple modiﬁcation t hat boosts exploration, both algorithms
can achieve the optimal regret of order /tildewideO(√
dT). On the other hand, due to their design, MLE-Cycle
and Semi-Myopic require the computation of O(√
T) andO(T) many MLEs, respectively, while ETC-
12
Doubling only requires O(log2(T)).In Section 5, we show via extensive numerical experiments that
ETC-Doubling achieves better statistical and computation al eﬃciency in ﬁnite-sample.
2.2.1 The supCB Algorithm
In Section S.3 of the supplement, we further propose supCB, w hich achieves a near-optimal /tildewideO(√
dT)
regret under assumptions weaker than the ones required in th e existing dynamic pricing literature. In
particular, it does not require Assumption 2.2, which impos es uniqueness of the true optimal price.
The supCBalgorithm is a more complex version of the widely us ed upper conﬁdence bound (UCB)
algorithm and is inspired by algorithms designed for the cla ssical contextual MAB problem (e.g. Auer,
2002; Chu et al., 2011; Li et al., 2017). The basis of supCB is a novel discovery of an intrinsic connec-
tion between contextual dynamic pricing and contextual MAB . By discretizing the one-dimensional
continuous action space [ l,u] withKequally-spaced price points, we can approximately treat the
contextual dynamic pricing problem as a contextual MAB with Karms. Importantly, we show that
regret due to the approximation error of such discretizatio n can be controlled at O(T/K).It is well-
known that the optimal regret of contextual MAB with Karms is/tildewideO(/radicalbig
dTlog(K)). Therefore, setting
K=O(/radicalbig
T/d) will intuitively provide a desired /tildewideO(√
dT) upper boundfor contextual dynamic pricing.
We refer to Section S.3of the supplement for more details of supCB.
Both supCBand ETCachieve the near-optimal regret /tildewideO(√
dT), with ETC oﬀering a slightly better
rate than supCB by a log( T) factor under an additional mild Assumption 2.2. However, ETC is much
simpler in its design with better computational eﬃciency. I n particular, ETC only requires a single
computation of MLE, while supCB requires the computation of at leastO(T) many MLE. Indeed,
extensive numerical experiments in Section S.2.1of the supplement indicate that ETC is superior
than supCB in both statistical and computational eﬃciency, where ETC gives an expected regret 3-4
times lower than supCB while using only 1/50 of its computati onal time. We refer to Section S.2.1
for details. Therefore, in practice, we recommend ETC over s upCB.
2.2.2 Dynamic pricing under adversarial contexts
In this paper, we focus on dynamic pricing under stochastic c ontexts, where the feature vector {zt}
is a sequence of i.i.d. random vectors with full-rank covari ance satisfying Assumption 2.1(a)-(b). To
13
our knowledge, all existing works in dynamic pricing under p arametric GLM demand models assume
stochastic contexts, with the exception of Wang et al. (2024 ), where the only assumption on {zt}is
that it is a sequence of bounded vectors (i.e. /bar⌈blzt/bar⌈bl ≤1), namely the adversarial contexts.
In general, for an online learning problem, the upper bound a lgorithms and minimax rates can be
substantially diﬀerent under stochastic and adversarial co ntexts. Nevertheless, for a more comprehen-
sive view, we provide a brief study of dynamic pricing under a dversarial contexts in Sections S.3.1 and
S.4 of the supplement. We give an overview of the results belo w.
Note that due to the adversarial contexts, the MLE /hatwideθτmay not be well-deﬁned as the design
matrixVτcan be rank-deﬁcient. Thus, we instead rely on the regulariz ed MLE /tildewideθτ, which is the unique
minimizer of the regularized (negative) log-likelihood −L(θ) +/bar⌈blθ/bar⌈bl2.In particular, based on /tildewideθτ, we
propose a modiﬁed supCB algorithm in Section S.3.1 and show t hat, under an additional assumption
on the link function ψ(·) of the GLM (which is satisﬁed by the linear demand model), it achieves the
near-optimal /tildewideO(√
dT) regret upper bound under adversarial contexts.
Furthermore, in Section S.4, we propose a novel UCB algorith m that works for a general GLM
demand model underadversarial contexts and matches the /tildewideO(d√
T) regret upperboundin Wang et al.
(2024). Notably, ouralgorithm requiresweaker assumption sandiscomputationally moreeﬃcient than
the one in Wang et al. (2024). The key novelty of our proposed U CB algorithm lies in its design of the
upper conﬁdence boundfor the true revenue function. In part icular, our upper conﬁdence boundtakes
aclosed-form by leveraging the structure of the revenue function r(p,zt,θ∗) and the convexity of the
link function ψ(·) of GLM (i.e. Assumption 2.1(e), which makes ψ′(·) a strictly increasing function).
We refer to the supplement for more detailed results of our pr oposed algorithms, theoretical guar-
antees, and numerical experiments that showcase the eﬃcien cy (in terms of both regret and compu-
tational time) of our algorithm compared to the algorithms i n Wang et al. (2024).
3 Contextual dynamic pricing under LDP constraints
As discussed in the introduction, there is a growing trend fo r implementing privacy preserving prac-
tice in business operations where personal information is u sed. This motivates us to further study
14
contextual dynamic pricing within the framework of LDP. We ﬁ rst introduce the notion of LDP for
contextual dynamic pricing, then propose the ETC-LDP algor ithm in Section 3.1and establish its
regret upper bound in Section 3.2.In Section 3.3, we further extend our study to contextual dyn amic
pricing under mixed privacy constraints, which bridges non -private and private dynamic pricing.
Fort∈[T], letst= (zt,yt,pt)betherawdataobservedattime t. Wefollow theformulation ofLDP
in dynamic pricing put forward by the seminal work Chen et al. (2022a), where the privacy-preserving
policy consists of a two-part structure that
pt∼At(·|zt,w1,···,wt−1) =At(zt,w<t), (9)
wt∼Qt(·|st,w1,···,wt−1) =Qt(st,w<t), (10)
withAtbeingthepricingstrategyand Qtbeingtheprivacymechanism. Here, wtdenotestheprivatized
statistic based on the raw data stand we denote w<t= (w1,···,wt−1) withw<0:=∅.
The pricing strategy At(zt,w<t) utilizes the true context ztof thetth customer and the privatized
information w<tfrom previous time periods. Note that while the raw context ztis utilized for pricing
purposes,itisneverretainedorstoredbytheseller. Inpar ticular, thepersonalizedprice ptiscomputed
based onztlocallyon the customer’s device and the only data passed to the selle r is the privatized wt.
Theprivatization mechanism Qt(st,w<t) is used to transform the raw data of the tth customer, st, into
a privatized statistic wt. Note that Qtonly utilizes the privatized statistics w<tand thus maintains
privacy for previously encountered consumers. We remark th at the seller does not store raw customer
datast, but only the privatized statistic wtis retained for future pricing. Following Duchi et al. (2018 )
and Chen et al. (2022a), we have the below deﬁnition.
Deﬁnition 3.1 (ǫ-LDP for dynamic pricing) .For anyǫ>0, a policyπ={Qt,At}T
t=1satisﬁesǫ-LDP
if for anyt∈[T],w<tandst,s′
t, we haveQt(W|st,w<t)≤eǫQt(W|s′
t,w<t), for any measurable set W.
We remark that our theoretical results hold for any ǫ∈(0,b], wherebis an absolute constant. For
simplicity, we assume in the following that ǫ∈(0,1] in all of our theoretical results regarding LDP.

3.1 The ETC-LDP algorithm
Algorithm 3presents the proposed ETC-LDP algorithm for privacy-prese rving contextual dynamic
pricing, with an L2-ball mechanism subroutine (Duchi et al., 2018) in Algorith m4. The high-level
structure of ETC-LDP is the same as the ETC algorithm in Secti on2.2for non-private dynamic
pricing. In particular, it partitions the entire horizon in to an exploration phase and an exploitation
phase. The exploration phase is used for privateparameter estimation while in the exploitation phase,
the seller uses a greedy approach to set optimal prices based on the estimated model parameter.
Unlike ETC where the seller can simply conduct a single maxim um likelihood estimation after
collecting all raw data {st}t∈[τ]in the exploration phase, an alternative estimator is neede d for ETC-
LDPtomeettheLDPconstraint. Inparticular, leveraging th esequential natureoftheLDPnotionand
dynamic pricing, we employ a stochastic gradient descent (S GD) based privacy-preserving estimation
procedure and integrate it into the exploration phase of ETC -LDP (i.e. Steps a1-a3 in Algorithm 3).

3.2 Regret upper bound of ETC-LDP
We start with a mild modeling assumption.
Assumption 3.1. (a) The parameter space Θ is compact and satisﬁes that θ∗∈Θ⊆ {θ:/bar⌈blθ−θ∗/bar⌈bl ≤
cθ}, for some absolute constant cθ>0. (b) There exist absolute constants cλ1,cλ2>0 such that
cλ1/d≤λz≤¯λz≤cλ2/d, where recall λz=λmin(Σz) and¯λz=λmax(Σz), with{zt}satisfying
Assumption 2.1and Σz=E(z1z⊤

Assumptions 2.1(c) and3.1(a) together ensure that {x⊤θ:x∈ Xandθ∈Θ}is bounded. Deﬁne

we have that the population log-likelihood function l(θ) :=E[l(θ;st)] in the exploration phase of
ETC-LDP is φz-strongly concave over Θ with φz:=Lpκ∗λz.This serves as the foundation for the
theoretical analysis of the SGD estimator /hatwideθτ. Note that if we assume /bar⌈blθ∗/bar⌈bl ≤cfor some absolute
17
constantc, Assumption 3.1(a) then holds by setting Θ = {θ:/bar⌈blθ/bar⌈bl ≤c}andcθ= 2c.
Assumption 3.1(b) imposes the mild condition that λz≍¯λz≍1/d. This essentially requires ztto
have a balanced covariance matrix, which is a standard assum ption in the statistical regression litera-
ture. Note that the 1 /dfactor is due to the fact that /bar⌈blzt/bar⌈bl ≤1 as in Assumption 2.1(a). Theorem 3.1
gives a high-probability upper bound on the estimation erro r for the private SGD estimator /hatwideθτin the
exploration phase of ETC-LDP, serving as the basis for bound ing the regret of ETC-LDP.

We remark that our private SGD estimator for the GLM is adapte d from a similar procedure
in Duchi et al. (2018) without the truncation mechanism. Ass uming the boundedness of the gradi-

a non-asymptotic high-probability bound for the private SG D estimator via substantially diﬀerent
technical arguments adapted from the standard SGD literatu re (Rakhlin et al., 2011). As we allow
an unbounded gradient, our result requires a careful analys is of the additional bias-variance trade-oﬀ
due to truncation. Our result matches the one in Duchi et al. ( 2018), up to logarithmic terms, with
log(1/δ) stemming from the high probability nature of our bound and l og(τ) from the truncation bias.

Remark 3.2 (Dynamic pricing under ( ǫ,∆)-LDP) .A relaxation of ǫ-LDP is (ǫ,∆)-LDP, where one
further allows a ∆ ∈(0,1] probability of privacy leakage (see Deﬁnition S.6.1 for m ore details). In
Section S.6.5 of the supplement, we propose an ETC-LDP-Appr ox algorithm for dynamic pricing un-
der (ǫ,∆)-LDP and show that it achieves the same order of regret uppe r bound as ETC-LDP under
ǫ-LDP (up to a O(/radicalbig
log(1.25/∆)) term), a common phenomenon in the LDP literature (Duchi e t al.,
19
2018). The key modiﬁcation is to replace the L2-ball privacy mechanism of ETC-LDP with a Gaus-
sian mechanism of a carefully calibrated variance. Technic ally, we extend the uniform concentration
inequality for martingale diﬀerence sequences in Rakhlin et al. (2011) from bounded random variables
to random variables with certain moment conditions (e.g. su b-Gaussian). This inequality is of inde-
pendent interest (see Lemma S.6.2). Numerical experiments are further conducted to show the similar
performance by ETC-LDP and ETC-LDP-Approx in ﬁnite-sample . Thus, in practice, we recommend
ETC-LDP as it oﬀers stronger privacy guarantees. We refer to S ection S.6.5 for more details.
3.3 Dynamic pricing under mixed privacy constraints
The ETC-LDP algorithm and its theoretical guarantees, as st udied in Sections 3.1 and 3.2, are de-
signed for the scenario where all users have the same level of privacy guarantees. However, in reality,
consumers may exhibit heterogeneous preferences towards t he trade-oﬀ between privacy and per-
sonalization, or simply diﬀerent attitudes towards privacy . To accommodate this, ﬁrms often oﬀer
consumers some ﬂexibility regarding their privacy levels, such as cookies consent banners, mobile apps
permissions, social media privacy settings, search engine privacy modes and e-commerce personal-
ization preferences, where users typically are given the ch oices to opt in or out of certain privacy
protection mechanism. This ﬂexibility and heterogeneity p resent both opportunities and challenges
in statistical privacy. For example, recent research has ex plored leveraging public data in privacy
analysis to improve utility and developing personalized le arning algorithms that adapt to individual
privacy preferences; see Cummings et al. (2024) and referen ces therein.
This motivate us to further extend our study to dynamic prici ng under mixedprivacy constraints.
In particular, we consider the scenario where arriving cons umers exhibit two levels of privacy pref-
erences: non-private, where they are willing to share raw in formationst= (zt,yt,pt) with the ﬁrm,
and private, where they require an ǫ-LDP protection of st. More speciﬁcally, we assume that the
consumer’s privacy preference is encoded by a random variab lemt∈ {0,1}wheremt= 0 indicates
a private consumer and mt= 1 indicates a non-private consumer, and there is an unknown mixture
probability p∗∈[0,1] such that each arriving consumer has p∗probability of being non-private and
20
1−p∗probability of being private with ǫ-LDP. In addition, {mt}is an i.i.d. process across consumers.
Same as before, we assume ǫ∈(0,1] for notational simplicity and remark that all results hol d for any
ǫ∈(0,b], wherebis an absolute constant.
Intuitively, thepresenceofnon-privateconsumers(regul ated byp∗)can helpimprovemodelestima-
tion forθ∗and thus attain a lower regret compared to dynamic pricing un derpureǫ-LDP constraints
(i.e.p∗≡0) in Section 3.1. Toachieve this, weproposetheETC-LDP-Mi xed algorithm inAlgorithm 5,
which is adapted from ETC-LDP in Algorithm 3 with several imp ortant modiﬁcations to eﬀectively
utilize the information from both private and non-private c onsumers.
First, thanks to the information from the non-private consu mers, we are able to explore less than
ETC-LDP. In particular, given p∗∈(0,1), the optimal exploration length now takes the form
τ(p∗) :=log(T)√
Td/radicalbig
p∗+(1−p∗)ǫ2/d. (13)
Recall as discussed following Theorem 3.1, ǫ2/dcan be viewed as the eﬀective sample size of an ǫ-
LDP private consumer. Since ǫ2/d≤1,τ(p∗) is a decreasing function of p∗. That is, having more
non-private consumers (thus higher quality data) leads to a shorter exploration length. Note that we
haveτ(1) =√
dTlog(T) forp∗= 1 andτ(0) =d√
Tlog(T)/ǫforp∗= 0, which recover the optimal
order of exploration length for ETC in Theorem 2.2 under the n on-private case and for ETC-LDP
in Theorem 3.2 under the ǫ-LDP private case, respectively. Therefore, τ(p∗) is intuitive and bridges
private and non-private dynamic pricing seamlessly.
In practice, p∗may be unknown, which further motivates the two-stage explo ration design for
ETC-LDP-Mixed. In Stage I exploration, which lasts for τ1=√

a private SGD and a non-private SGD to eﬀectively utilize the i nformation from both private and
non-private consumers.

can be observed even with a small p∗such as 0.05 or 0.1.
4 Minimax lower bounds on the regret
In this section, we verify the optimality of our proposed sup CB, ETC and ETC-LDP algorithms.
Theorem 4.1 (Non-private lower bound) .LetPbe the collection of distributions satisfying the GLM
model in ( 1), Assumptions 2.1and2.2, and Π be the set of all non-anticipating policies. There exi sts
an absolute constant /tildewidec>0 such that inf π∈Πsupv∈PEπ
vRT≥/tildewidec√
dT, for allT≥4d2log(T).
Theorem 4.1shows that both supCB and ETC achieve the minimax optimal reg ret, up to logarith-
mic factors. The sub-linear dependence on the dimensionali tydis the ﬁrst time seen in the contextual
dynamic pricing literature and extends the Ω(√
T) lower bound for contextual-free dynamic pricing in
Broder and Rusmevichientong (2012). Unlike Broder and Rusm evichientong (2012), which connects
the regret with the error of a hypothesis testing problem bet ween two highly indistinguishable de-
mand models and applies Le Cam’s lemma, our technical argume nts are substantially diﬀerent and
more involved. In particular, to capture the eﬀect of the dime nsiond, we construct 2ddemand models
that can be grouped into pairs, where each pair is highly indi stinguishable along one dimension of
the context vector. We connect the regret with the error of a m ultiple-classiﬁcation problem and
establish the lower bound based on Assouad’s method. We rema rk that due to adaptivity of the
data (i.e. {yt,zt,pt}generated by the pricing policy are not i.i.d.), our argumen ts are more involved
than standard applications of Assouad’s method in lower bou nds for statistical estimation problems.
Theorem 4.2 (Private lower bound) .LetPbe the collection of distributions satisfying the GLM
23
modelin ( 1), Assumptions 2.1,2.2and3.1, andΠ(ǫ) betheset of all non-anticipating ǫ-LDPpolicies as
deﬁned in Deﬁnition 3.1. There exists an absolute constant /tildewidec>0 such that inf π∈Π(ǫ)supv∈PEπ
vRT≥
/tildewidecd√
T/ǫ, for allT≥/tildewidec′·d4ǫ−2log(T), where /tildewidec′>0 is an absolute constant.
Theorem 4.2shows that ETC-LDP achieves the minimax optimal regret, up t o logarithmic factors.
Compared with Theorem 4.1, the regret lower bound under LDP inﬂates by a factor of√
d/ǫ, and the
condition on Tis more stringent, echoing observations in the privacy lite rature (e.g. Cai et al., 2021).
The proof of Theorem 4.2shares some similarity with that of Theorem 4.1, with the Assouad’s method
replaced by a private version tailored for LDP. While this st rategy has been used in the LDP literature
for establishing lower bounds for private estimation with i ndependent data (e.g. Duchi et al., 2018;
Acharya et al., 2024), our setting is much more involved and t hus requires more delicate analysis. In
particular, in our lower bound setting, we allow the data (es pecially price) to be adaptively generated
byanypricingpolicy π, makingthedatainherentlydependent. Weprovideasketch o fproofbelow and
refer to Section S.7.1 of the supplement for a detailed roadm ap of proof and technical contributions.
Sketch of proof for Theorem 4.2. The proof is based on the construction of 2dhighly indistin-
guishable revenue functions, where the details of the const ruction can be found in Section S.7.2. Given
this setup, the remainder of the proof consists of two key com ponents.
S1. (Lower bounding the regret by classiﬁcation errors). We ﬁrst apply a general reduction to relate
the regret bound to the classiﬁcation error in certain class iﬁcation problems. In particular,
inspired by Chen et al. (2022a), we show that for any non-anti cipatingǫ-LDP policy π, its
regret can be lower bounded by the classiﬁcation errors of dclassiﬁcation problems subject to
2ǫ-LDP constraints. This reduction is established in Lemma S. 7.4.
S2. (A new sharp lower bound on classiﬁcation errors) At a hig h level, we further lower bound the
classiﬁcation errors in terms of the Hellinger distance bet ween pairs of distributions, with the
Hellinger distance itself being upper bounded using the inf ormation contraction bound tech-
niques developed in Acharya et al. (2024). Importantly, we m ake non-trivial modiﬁcations to
adapt the technical tools in Acharya et al. (2024), which is d esigned for statistical estimation
24
problems under LDP with i.i.d. data, to our setting of dynami c pricing under LDP, where the
data can be adaptively collected by anynon-anticipating pricing policy πand thus exhibit in-
herent dependencies. To address this, we develop a novel con ditioning argument via martingale
decomposition when bounding the Hellinger distance betwee n joint distributions, making the
intrinsic dependence of the data tractable for analysis. Th is step is established in Lemma S.7.8.
We remark that our lower bound analysis in Theorem 4.2 is new a nd ﬁrst time seen in the dynamic
pricing literature (in particular Lemmas S.7.8 and S.7.9). In fact, existing lower bound construction in
contextual bandit (Han et al., 2021) and contextual (non-pa rametric) dynamic pricing (Chen et al.,
2022a) under LDP constraints mainly utilize the well-known private Le Cam Lemma and private
Assouad’s Lemma developed in Duchi et al. (2018), combined w ith KL divergence bounds. However,
we ﬁnd this strategy can only provide a Ω(√
dT/ǫ) lower bound for our setting, which does not fully
characterize the cost of privacy in dimension d.Instead, our proof strategy is to leverage the Hellinger
distance, which is sharper than the KL divergence, and adapt the information contraction bound in
Acharya et al. (2024) to derive the optimal Ω( d√
T/ǫ) bound. This proof strategy is the ﬁrst time
seen in the literature for online learning under LDP constra ints and thus is of independent interest.

5.3 Real data analysis
Inthis section, we explorepractical utility of thepropose dalgorithms on areal-world auto loan dataset
provided by the Center for Pricing and Revenue Management at Columbia University.
The dataset records all auto loan applications received by a major online lender in the U.S. from
July 2002 to November 2004 with a total of 208,085 applicatio ns. For each application, we observe
information about the loan (e.g. term and amount), the prosp ective consumer (e.g. FICO score), and
the economic environment (e.g. prime rate). We also observe the monthly payment required for the
approved loan, which can be viewed as the pricing decision by the company, and whether the oﬀer
was accepted – a binary purchasing decision by a consumer.
We follow thefeatureselection resultinLuo et al. (2021) an dBastani et al. (2022) and usetheloan
amount approved, term, prime rate, the competitor’s rate an d FICO score as covariates, i.e. d= 5.We
refer to Table S.2in the supplement for detailed description of the covariate s. We further standardize
each covariate by its sample mean. The price pof a loan is computed as the net present value of future
payment minus the loan amount, i.e. p= Monthly Payment ×/summationtextTerm
i=1(1 + Rate)−i−Loan Amount .
31
We set Rate as 0 .12%, an average of the monthly London interbank oﬀered rate fo r the studied time
period. For convenience, we use one thousand dollars ($1000 ) as the basic unit for loan prices.
Note that it is impossible to obtain consumers’ real online r esponses to any pricing strategy unless
they were used in the system when the data were collected. The refore, following the literature, we ﬁrst
estimate the demand model based on the entire observations a nd useit as the ground truth to generate
consumer responses. In particular, denote the tth consumer decision as yt, the covariates as zt∈R5,
and loan price as pt, we ﬁt the logistic regression model in ( 15) to{yt,zt,pt}208085

6 Conclusion
In this paper, we studied the contextual dynamic pricing pro blem with a GLM demand model and a
stochastic contextual vector of dimension d. For the non-private version, we propose two algorithms,
supCB and ETC, that can achieve the optimal regret of order /tildewideO(√
dT). Importantly, this improves
upon the state-of-the-art in the literature by a factor of√
d. In addition, we consider the timely
problem of dynamic pricing under LDP and propose a private SG D based ETC-LDP algorithm, which
gives a regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T/ǫ), highlighting the cost of privacy. We further establish lo wer bounds
andillustrate theutility of theproposedmethodsviaexten sive numerical experiments. Oneinteresting
direction for future research is to consider scenarios wher e there are unknown changes in the context
distribution or the parameters of the GLM demand model, whic h can be more realistic in practice.

S.1 Literature review
Three streams of literature are closely related to our paper : dynamic pricing with demand learning,
contextual multi-armed bandit, and diﬀerential privacy for online learning.
S.1.1 Dynamic pricing with demand learning
Due to the increasing popularity of online retailing, dynam ic pricing with demand learning has
been extensively studied across statistics, machine learn ing, and operations research. Early works
in dynamic pricing mainly focus on the context-free setting , see e.g. Besbes and Zeevi (2009) and
Broder and Rusmevichientong (2012). In particular, Broder and Rusmevichientong (2012) show that
there exist two regimes in context-free dynamic pricing, th e well-separated case where demand curves
1
donotoverlapatanypriceandthegeneralcasewherethereex istsanuninformativeprice. Broder and Rusmevichientong
(2012) show that the optimal regret for the well-separated case is O(log(T)) and for the general case
isO(√
T).
For parametric contextual dynamic pricing, Qiang and Bayati (2016) consider the well-separated
case with a linear demand function and derive a regret upper b ound of order O(dlog(T)), which is fur-
ther corroborated by Javanmard and Nazerzadeh (2019), with additional results in high-dimensional
covariates cases. For the general case, Ban and Keskin (2021) adapt the MLE-cycle algorithm pro-
posed in Broder and Rusmevichientong (2012) and achieve a regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T) assuming GLM
demand functions. Allowing for adversarial contexts, Wang et al. (2024) propose a UCB algorithm
and derive the same /tildewideO(d√
T) rate for the regret under GLM demand functions assumptions .
Beyond parametric models, Chen and Gallego (2021) study contextual dynamicpricing undernon-
parametric demand models, while Wang et al. (2023),Luo et al. (2024) andFan et al. (2024) study
semiparametric cases. In particular, Fan et al. (2024) show that under a linear valuation model and
an inﬁnitely diﬀerentiable market noise function, the optim al regret is of order /tildewideO(√
dT), which in some
sense complements our result under the parametric GLM model . Contextual dynamic pricing has also
been studied from a wide range of aspects, such as consumer st rategic behavior ( Liu et al. ,2023),
non-stationarity ( Zhao et al. ,2023), fairness constraints ( Chen et al. ,2023), and diﬀerential privacy
constraints (see later for more detailed discussion).
S.1.2 Contextual multi-armed bandit
Most contextual MAB literature focuses on the linear contex tual bandit setting where the reward
assumes a linear model with rt,a=x⊤
t,aθ+ǫt, wherext,a∈Xis the context associated with arm a,
θis the unknown parameter and ǫtis the error process. It is well-known that the optimal regre t is
of order/tildewideO(d√
T) when the arm space Xis a compact (but otherwise arbitrary) subset of Rdand
can be achieved via UCB ( Dani et al. ,2008;Abbasi-Yadkori et al. ,2011). In contrast, for the setting
where the number of available arms Kin each round is ﬁnite, the regret can be further improved to
/tildewideO(/radicalbig
dTlog(K)) (Auer,2002;Chu et al. ,2011). However, such a regret bound can only be achieved
2
via a more involved conﬁdence bound-type algorithm. This ty pe of algorithm is ﬁrst proposed by
Auer(2002) under the name sup-LinRel and is extended to the generalize d linear bandit in Li et al.
(2017), which inspires the design of the proposed supCB algorithm in our paper. Note that contextual
dynamic pricing can in some sense be viewed as a contextual MA B with inﬁnite number of arms,
as the price can take any value in [ l,u].However, the key insight of our work is that, despite being
uncountable, this action space is of dimension oneregardless of the contextual dimensionality d, and
thus can be well-approximated by a contextual MAB with reasonably many arms via discretization
and achieves an optimal regret of order /tildewideO(√
dT).
S.1.3 Diﬀerential privacy for online learning
The most prevalent privacy concept used in both academia and industry is the notion of diﬀerential
privacy (DP) proposed by Dwork et al. (2006). In the DP literature, there are two widely used
privacy schemes known as central diﬀerential privacy (CDP, Dwork et al. ,2006) and local diﬀerential
privacy (LDP, Evﬁmievski et al. ,2003).
CDP assumes the existence of a trusted central handler (e.g. the ﬁrm) that can securely store and
process raw consumer information. This may raise concerns a bout data vulnerability, particularly the
risk of attacks on the central server, leading to unauthoriz ed access to sensitive customer information.
In contrast, LDP oﬀers a decentralized approach that priorit izes individual privacy. Instead of relying
on a central authority to store and process data, LDP allows e ach customer to locally randomize their
data before sharing them with the ﬁrm or any other party.
Due to the practical importance of privacy, there is a recent surge in designing online learning algo-
rithms with DP guarantees for contextual MAB and contextual dynamic pricing. Shariﬀ and Sheﬀet
(2018) study linear contextual bandits under CDP, while Zheng et al. (2020) explore them under LDP.
Based on a covariate-diversity assumption, Han et al. (2021) propose an exploration-free algorithm for
generalized linear bandits under LDP. However, this assump tion in general does not hold under dy-
namic pricing as the adaptively chosen price is part of the co variate.
Chen et al. (2022b) examine dynamic pricing under a GLM demand model with CDP co nstraints
3
andChen et al. (2022a) study nonparametric dynamic pricing under both LDP and CDP constraints.
Under the oﬄine setting, where all historical data are acces sible for learning, Lei et al. (2023) study
contextual dynamic pricing for a linear demand model under L DP, focusing primarily on preserving
the privacy of contexts. Leveraging the structure of the lin ear model, they propose a private ordinary
least squares estimator, which cannot be used for GLM. In thi s paper, we consider contextual dynamic
pricingwithaGLMdemandfunctionundertheonlinesettinga ndproposeastochasticgradientdescent
based algorithm that oﬀers LDP guarantees for both consumer r esponses and contexts.

Implementation details . For ETC-LDP, the implementation follows that in Section 5.2.3, while to
handle the unknown horizon T, we use doubling trick. In particular, for the qth episode, which is
of lengthTq= 2q, we set the number of price experiments as τq= min/braceleftbig
Tq,d/radicalbig
2TqlogTq/ǫ/bracerightbig

recallǫis the privacy parameter. To be more realistic, we set Θ = {θ:/⌊ard⌊lθ−θ◦/⌊ard⌊l≤√
d}, where for
each experiment, θois uniformly sampled from the unit ball centered at θ∗.In other words, θois a
perturbed version of θ∗. Since ETC-LDP requires more price experiments, we set the p rice range [l,u]
to be [0,3] for price experiments and keep the price range to be [0 ,10] for price exploitation, which
improves its performance.
S.3 The supCB algorithm
As discussed in the main text, a key element of supCB is to appr oximate the contextual dynamic
pricing problem via contextual MAB based on discretization . In particular, we approximate the price
interval [l,u], which is a one-dimensional continuous action space, by a s et ofKequispaced price
pointsPK={p(a)}a∈[K], wherep(a)=l+ (a−1)(u−l)/(K−1) fora∈[K]. We thus have a
generalized linear bandit (GLB) with Karms, where xt,a= (z⊤
t,−p(a)·z⊤
t)⊤is the context associated
with arma∈[K] at timet.We name it revenue-GLB. Unlike the standard GLB (e.g. Filippi et al. ,
2010;Li et al.,2017), where the reward is deﬁned as the conditional mean E(yt|xt,a) =ψ′(x⊤
t,aθ∗), our
reward here is the more complicated revenue function r(p(a),zt,θ∗) =p(a)·ψ′(x⊤
t,aθ∗).The optimal
9
arm at time tis therefore the arm associated with po
t∈argmaxp∈PKr(p,zt,θ∗).
Denoteπas a policy for the revenue-GLB based on PK. By simple algebra, its regret for the
original contextual dynamic pricing problem as deﬁned in ( 4) can be rewritten as
Rπ

Importantly, Lemma S.5.2in the supplement shows that the class of revenue functions {r(p,z,θ∗) :
z∈ Z}is uniformly Lr-Lipschitz continuous with Lr:=Mψ1+uubMψ2. This implies that the
approximation error is of order O(T/K) and thus can be easily controlled.
Therefore, the key to an optimal regret Rπ
Tis an algorithm that can eﬃciently bound the regret
of the revenue-GLB. We then propose supCB in Algorithm S.2, with a CB-GLM subroutine in Algo-
rithmS.1. Similar to standard UCB algorithms (e.g. Lai and Robbins ,1985), the idea of supCB is
to balance exploration and exploitation via conﬁdence boun ds. However, to achieve the near-optimal
/tildewideO(√
dT) regret, supCB is substantially more involved than the stan dard UCB in its algorithm design.
We ﬁrst discuss the CB-GLM subroutine. In particular, based on an observed sample {yt,xt}t∈Ψ,
CB-GLM ﬁrst estimates θ∗via the MLE /hatwideθand constructs the design matrix V=/summationtext
t∈Ψxtx⊤
t. It then
provides an estimator ra=p(a)·ψ′(x⊤
a/hatwideθ) and a conﬁdence bound wa=α/⌊ard⌊lxa/⌊ard⌊lV−1, for the expected
revenuer(p(a),z,θ∗) =p(a)·ψ′(x⊤
aθ∗) of armxa= (z⊤,−p(a)z⊤)⊤at a new context z. Note that given
Theorem 2.1(iii), by the smoothness of ψ′(·) and boundedness of p(a), we have that wacan serve as a
valid high probability conﬁdence bound for |ra−r(p(a),z,θ∗)|with an appropriately chosen α.

However, Theorem 2.1requires the crucial assumption that {yt}t∈Ψareconditionally independent
given{xt}t∈Ψ. Suchanassumptioningeneral doesnot holdwhenthesample {yt,xt}t∈Ψareadaptively
10
generated based on standard bandit algorithms such as UCB, a s the outcome of ytwill inﬂuence the
future arm choice xt′fort′>t.This issue is solved via a decomposition technique developed in Auer
(2002). This technique is previously used in linear contextual ba ndits (e.g. Auer,2002;Chu et al. ,
2011) and GLB (e.g. Li et al.,2017) to create conditionally independent samples.
In supCB (Algorithm S.2), we adapt this technique to the revenue-GLB. In particular , in each
roundt, supCB decomposes its decision process into S=⌊log2(T)⌋stages. At stage s∈[S], in
step I, we ﬁrst call CB-GLM (based on samples in Fs∪Ψs) to compute the estimated revenue r(s)
t,a
and conﬁdence bound w(s)
t,afor all remaining candidate arms. In step II, if w(s)
t,a>2−sfor somea(i.e.
uncertainty is high), supCB will choose arm afor exploration, collect the result in Ψ sand move to
roundt+1. Otherwise, step III checks whether all w(s)
t,a≤1/√
Tand if satisﬁed, we simply pick the
arm with highest r(s)
t,aand move to round t+1 as no exploration is needed. If not, the arms are ﬁltered

one. Since all w(s)
t,a≤2−sin step IV and w(s)
t,ais a high probability bound on |r(s)

t,j−2·2−s. Note that this decision process
is guaranteed to terminate in at most Sstages since 1 /√

Denote Ψ s(t) as the sample collected in stage sbefore round tand denote Ψo

key property of supCB is that due to the decomposition design , whether an arm xt,ais selected in

independence of{yi}i∈Ψos(t)given{xi}i∈Ψos(t)and we refer to Lemma S.5.3for the formal proof.
Note that supCBstarts witha pureexploration phaseduringw hichptis drawnuniformly from PK.

12
Theorem S.3.1states that when the sample size Tis suﬃciently large, supCB can achieve a regret
of orderO(√
dTlog3/2(T)), which is optimal up to logarithmic terms (see the lower bo und Ω(√
dT)
in Theorem 4.1later). To our knowledge, this improves the best available r egret in the contextual
dynamic pricing literature (e.g. Ban and Keskin ,2021;Wang et al. ,2024) by a factor of√
d.
As discussed in the introduction, there are two types of cont extual MAB problems. For the
ﬁrst type, its arm space Xis ad-dimensional (uncountable) compact subset of Rdand the op-
timal regret /tildewideO(d√
T) can be achieved via standard UCB ( Dani et al. ,2008;Abbasi-Yadkori et al. ,
2011). The second type has a ﬁnite Knumber of arms in each round t, where the optimal regret is
/tildewideO(/radicalbig
dTlog(K)) (Auer,2002;Chu et al. ,2011). The key insight of Theorem S.3.1and supCB is that
the contextual dynamic pricing problem can be essentially v iewed as a contextual MAB of the second
type. The intuition is that, despite being uncountable, the action space [ l,u] of dynamic pricing is
of dimension oneregardless of the context dimension d. Moreover, thanks to the uniform Lipschitz
continuity of the revenue function, we can discretize the ac tion space [ l,u] intoKarms and achieve a
controllable approximation error as long as Kis of polynomial order in T(i.e.O(√
T), independent
ofd).
Unknown T .WhenTisunknown,werunsupCBwiththestandarddoublingtrick(e. g.Auer et al. ,
1995) in the bandit literature. In particular, we partition N+into non-overlapping episodes, with the
kth episode of length Ek= 2k. We run Algorithm S.2withT=Ekfor thekth episode and then
restart at the next episode. To conserve space, we refer to Th eoremS.5.1for its theoretical guarantee.
See also Algorithm 2later for a detailed implementation of ETC with the doubling trick.
S.3.1 A modiﬁed supCB for adversarial contexts
In the following, we propose a (slightly) modiﬁed supCB algo rithm based on regularized MLE for
adversarial contexts. It requires a stronger assumption on the convexity of the link function ψ(·) as is
documented in Assumption S.4.1(b). In particular, a linear demand model satisﬁes this assu mption
asψ(x) = 1/2x2in such case.
Assumption S.3.1. (a) The feature vector {zt}t∈[T]⊂Z ⊆{z∈Rd:/⌊ard⌊lz/⌊ard⌊l≤1}is an arbitrary
13
sequence of bounded vectors. (b) The function ψ(·) is three times continuously diﬀerentiable and its
second order derivative ψ′′(a)≥cψ>0 for alla∈R, wherecψ≤1 is an absolute constant. (c) The
true model parameter is bounded with /⌊ard⌊lθ∗/⌊ard⌊l≤cθfor some absolute constant cθ.
Non-asymptotic bounds for regularized MLE : Forτ∈N+, given a sample{(yt,xt)}t∈[τ], the
log-likelihood of the GLM takes the form L(θ) =/summationtextτ

tθ−ψ(x⊤
tθ)}. We deﬁne the regularized
(negative) log-likelihood function

A modiﬁed supCB algorithm : Based on Lemma S.3.1, we modify the CB-GLM subroutine in
Algorithm S.1into a regularized version. In particular, instead of using the log-likelihood L(θ) and

regret of order /tildewideO(√

The proof of Theorem S.3.2follows the same structure as that of Theorem S.3.1. In particular,
the results in Lemmas S.5.2,S.5.3andS.5.5continue to hold for the modiﬁed supCB algorithm. The

Theorem 2.1(c)), which is straightforward. The detailed proof is thus o mitted to conserve space.

S.4 Dynamic pricing under adversarial contexts
In this section, we further study dynamic pricing under adve rsarial contexts. In particular, we relax
the stochastic contexts condition in Assumption 2.1(a)-(b) of the main text to Assumption S.4.1,
where we only assume {zt}is a sequence of bounded vectors.
16
Assumption S.4.1. (a) The feature vector {zt}t∈[T]⊂Z ⊆{z∈Rd:/⌊ard⌊lz/⌊ard⌊l≤1}is an arbitrary
sequence of bounded vectors. (b). The parameter space Θ is co mpact such that/⌊ard⌊lΘ/⌊ard⌊l≤cθ.
Due to the adversarial nature of {zt}, the design matrix of the log-likelihood function L(θ) can
be rank deﬁcient. Therefore, for model estimation, we inste ad rely on the regularized negative log-
likelihood function
/tildewideLt(θ) =−/braceleftiggt/summationdisplay

2,
whereλ>0 is a constant (e.g. λ= 1).
Algorithm S.3proposes a novelly designed UCB algorithm for dynamic prici ng under the adversar-
ial contexts. We further establish its theoretical guarant ees in Theorem S.4.1, where we show that it
can achieve a regret upper bound of order /tildewideO(d√
T) under adversarial contexts, matching prior works
in the literature ( Wang et al. ,2024).

Theorem S.4.1. Suppose Assumption 2.1(c)-(e) and Assumption S.4.1hold. Setλ= 1 andα=
BA1/radicalbig
dlog(T(1+u2)/δ). We have for any T >0, the regret of UCB is upper bounded by RT≤
BA2·d√
Tlog(T/δ), whereBA1,BA2>0 are absolute constants that only depend on quantities in
Assumption 2.1(c)-(e) and Assumption S.4.1.
The key novelty of our proposed UCB algorithm lies in its desi gn of the upperconﬁdence boundfor
the revenue function (i.e. ( S.5)), which takes a much simpler structure than existing UCB al gorithms
in prior works ( Wang et al. (2024), see below for more discussions) and thus is computational ly more
17
eﬃcient. In particular, simple algebra shows that the upper conﬁdence bound U(p,zt) in (S.5) takes
the formU(p,zt) =p·ψ′/parenleftbig
at0+at1p+at2p2/parenrightbig
, for someat0,at1,at2, which is a one-dimensional function
ofpand can therefore be optimized eﬃciently.
Intuition of our UCB : We now give some intuition of our UCB U(p,zt) in (S.5). Recall that the
true revenue function takes the form r(p,zt,θ∗) =p·ψ′(x(p)⊤
tθ∗), where we denote x(p)
t= (z⊤
t,−pz⊤
t)⊤.
A key observation is that due to the structure of r(p,zt,θ∗) and the fact that p>0, an upper bound
ofψ′(x(p)⊤
tθ∗) directly leads to a upper bound of r(p,zt,θ∗). Moreover, due to the intrinsic convexity
of GLM (i.e. ψ′′(·)>0, see Assumption 2.1(e)), we have ψ′(·) is a strictly increasing function and thus
an upper bound of x(p)⊤
tθ∗directly leas to an upper bound of ψ′(x(p)⊤
tθ∗). This argument leads to our
upper conﬁdence bound U(p,zt), as we show that, with a properly chosen parameter α, it holds that
x(p)⊤
t/hatwideθt+α/⌊ard⌊lx(p)
t/⌊ard⌊lV−1
t−1is a high probability upper bound for x(p)⊤
tθ∗for allp∈[l,u] andt∈[T].
Comparison with existing works : In the literature, Wang et al. (2024) is the only work
that considers dynamic pricing under adversarial contexts with GLM demand models. In particu-
lar,Wang et al. (2024) proposes a (computationally diﬃcult) UCB algorithm and sh ows its regret
upper bound is /tildewideO(d√
T).Their UCB takes the form (in our notation)
pt= argmax
p∈[l,u]/tildewideU(p,zt) := max
θ=(α,β)∈Ctp·ψ′(z⊤
tα−z⊤
tβp) (S.6)
whereCt={θ∈Θ :/⌊ard⌊lθ−/hatwideθt/⌊ard⌊lVt−1≤α}is the conﬁdence set for θat timet. As noted by Wang et al.
(2024), the inner optimization of /tildewideU(p,zt) is a 2d-dimensional non-convex optimization and is diﬃcult
to solve. To bypass the computational diﬃculty, the authors propose to approximate the optimization
in (S.6) via a heuristic Monte Carlo approach, which then incurs a wo rse regret upper bound than
/tildewideO(d√
T) and thus is unsatisfactory.
An an alternative, Wang et al. (2024) further proposes a Thompson Sampling based algorithm
that is computationally feasible and achieves the /tildewideO(d√
T) regret upper bound. However, it requires a
strong assumption (see Assumption 4 therein) that there exi sts an absolute constant g>0 such that
inf
z∈Z,p∈[l,u],θ∈/tildewideΘψ′′(z⊤α−z⊤βp)>g>0,
where/tildewideΘ =/braceleftbig
θ∈R2d:/⌊ard⌊lθ−θ∗/⌊ard⌊l2≤c·√dlogT/bracerightbig
for some constant c >0.Note this may not hold
18
even for logistic regression since /tildewideΘ isnotcompact but expands with d,T. In addition, our numerical
experiments in the next section show that our UCB algorithm i s computationally more eﬃcient than
the TS algorithm in Wang et al. (2024).
S.4.1 Numerical experiments
In this section, we conduct numerical experiments to compar e the ﬁnite sample performance and
computational time of our proposed UCB algorithm in Algorit hmS.3and the Thompson Sampling
based algorithm (hereafter TS-WTL) in Wang et al. (2024). Note that we choose not to implement
the UCB algorithm in Wang et al. (2024) since it requires a 2 d-dimensional non-convex optimization
and thus is computationally highly demanding. Moreover, th e numerical result in Wang et al. (2024)
indicates that their heuristic Monte Carlo based UCB algori thm has very similar performance in terms
of regret as their Thompson Sampling based algorithm.
For the demand model, we follow Section 5.1of the main text and consider the logistic regression
setting as in ( 15) with simulation scenario (S1), where α∗= 1.6·1d/√
dandβ∗=1d/√
d. However,
the feature vector {zt= (zt,1,···,zt,d)⊤}tare not i.i.d. but adversarial. In particular, we follow the
setting in Wang et al. (2024) and consider two scenarios.
(A1). Fort≤T/2, we setzt= (z∗
t,1.5·1d/2)/√
d, and fort>T/2, we setzt= (1.5·1d/2,z∗
t)/√
d,
wherez∗
tis ad/2-dimensional random vector with {z∗
t,i}d/2
i=1being i.i.d. uniform(0 ,3) random variables.
(A2). For allt∈[T], we setzt= (z∗
t,1.5·1d/2)/√
d, wherez∗
tis ad/2-dimensional random vector
with{z∗
t,i}d/2
i=1being i.i.d. uniform(0 ,3) random variables.
By design, the feature vector {zt}is adversarial under both (A1) and (A2), where its covarianc e
matrix is always of rank d/2 at any time t∈[T], making accurate estimation of the model parameter
infeasible. We set the conﬁdence parameter as α=√dlogTlog(logT)/10 for the UCB algorithm
in Algorithm S.3and follow Wang et al. (2024) for the implementation of the TS-WTL algorithm.
Similar to Wang et al. (2024), we set the horizon T∈ {1000,2500,5000,7500,10000}and set the
dimensiond∈{4,8,12,16}.

TS-WTL, given a ﬁxed dimension d, the regret scales sublinearly with respect to T, providing numer-
ical evidence for Theorem S.4.1. In addition, given a ﬁxed horizon T, the regret of both algorithms

S.5.3 Technical details in Section 2.2
Proposition S.5.2. DeﬁneRςo=/braceleftbig
(α⊤z,β⊤z) :z∈¯Z,/⌊ard⌊l(α⊤,β⊤)⊤−θ∗/⌊ard⌊l≤ςo/bracerightbig
.Under Assumptions
2.1(a), (c), (e) and 2.2, there exists a bivariate Lipschitz continuous function ϕ(·,·) overRςowith a
Lipschitz constant Cϕ>0 such that
p∗= argmax
p∈[l,u]/braceleftig
pψ′(α⊤z−β⊤zp)/bracerightig
=ϕ(α⊤z,β⊤z),
for allz∈¯Zandθ∈{(α⊤,β⊤) :/⌊ard⌊lθ−θ∗/⌊ard⌊l≤ςo}⊂R2d. Moreover, Cϕis an absolute constant that
only depends on quantities in Assumptions 2.1and2.2.
Proof of Proposition S.5.2.By deﬁnition,
p∗= argmax
p∈[l,u]{p·ψ′(α⊤z−β⊤zp)}.
Thus,p∗depends on θandzviaα⊤zandβ⊤z.
By Assumption 2.2, for allz∈¯Zandθ= (α,β) where/⌊ard⌊lθ−θ∗/⌊ard⌊l≤ςo, the optimal price p∗is
unique. Therefore, for all ( α⊤z,β⊤z)∈Rςo, we have that p∗is in fact a function of α⊤zandβ⊤zand
34
can be written as p∗=ϕ(α⊤z,β⊤z). Note it is clear that Rςois a compact set in R2and furthermore
Rςo⊆{(w,v) :|w|≤ua+ςo,|v|≤ub+ςo}by Assumption 2.1(a) and (c).
We next prove that ϕ(w,v) is continuous over Rςoby Berge’s Maximum Theorem ( Berge,1957).
To apply Berge’s Maximum Theorem, we ﬁrst deﬁne Γ( w,v) = [l,u] for anyw,v. Note that Γ( w,v)
is a set-valued function and is continuous at any ( w,v). By the deﬁnition of ϕ(w,v), we have that
ϕ(w,v) = argmax
p∈[l,u]{p·ψ′(w−vp)}= argmax
p∈Γ(w,v){p·ψ′(w−vp)}.
By Assumption 2.1(e),p·ψ′(w−vp) is a continuous function of ( p,w,v). Together with the fact
that Γ(w,v) is continuous at any ( w,v), by Berge’s Maximum Theorem, we have that ϕ(w,v) (as a
set-valued function) is upper hemicontinuous at any ( w,v). Moreover, by the above discussion, we
know thatϕ(w,v) is indeed a function over Rςo. Thus, by the deﬁnition of upper hemicontinuity, we
have thatϕ(w,v) is continuous over Rςo.
We now further show that ϕ(w,v) is a continuously diﬀerentiable function over Rςobased on an
application of the implicit function theorem. For any ﬁxed ( wo,vo)∈Rςo, by Assumption 2.2, there is
a unique optimal price p∗∈(l,u).Note that by Assumption 2.1(e), the revenue function r(p;wo,vo) =
p·ψ′(wo−vop) is twice continuously diﬀerentiable. Deﬁne f(p;wo,vo) =ψ′(wo−vop)−pψ′(wo−vop)vo,
which is thederivative of r(p;wo,vo).Sincep∗is the uniquemaximizer of r(p;wo,vo), we therefore have
thatf(p∗;wo,vo) = 0 andf′(p∗;wo,vo)<0. Therefore, viewing f(p;w,v) =ψ′(w−vp)−pψ′(w−vp)v
as a trivariate function of ( w,v,p), by the implicit function theorem, we have that there exist s a
continuously diﬀerentiable function g(w,v) (that may depend on ( wo,vo)) such that for all points
(w,v) in a small neighborhood of ( uo,vo), we have that f(g(w,v),w,v) = 0.By deﬁnition, this g(w,v)
is the optimal price p∗at (w,v). On the other hand, by our above result, we know that p∗=ϕ(w,v).
Therefore, we have that ϕ(w,v) =g(w,v) for all (w,v) inRςoand thusϕ(w,v) is continuously
diﬀerentiable over Rςo, which completes the proof.

S.5.4 The MLE-cycle algorithm and a simple modiﬁcation
The MLE-Cycle algorithm is ﬁrst proposed in Broder and Rusmevichientong (2012) for dynamic pric-
ing under an unknown demand model with no covariates (i.e. co ntext-free) and is shown to provide a
regret of order O(√
T). We summarize MLE-Cycle in Algorithm S.4. A (slight) variant of MLE-Cycle
is further introduced in Ban and Keskin (2021) for contextual dynamic pricing with a d-dimensional
covariate and is shown to provide a regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T).
The key idea of the MLE-Cycle algorithm is to divide the horiz onN+into cycles of increasing
lengths. In particular, the cth cycle is of length c+k, wherekis a constant. Within each cycle, the
ﬁrstkrounds are allocated for price experiments and the next crounds are used for price exploitation.
By simple algebra, it is easy to see that for all suﬃciently la rgeT,the number of price experiments
conducted till the Tth round is of order O(k√
T).
In the above mentioned literature, kis set as an absolute constant (typically 2) and MLE-Cycle
gives a regret of order O(d√
T), which is sub-optimal - supportedby Theorem 4.1. Theintuitive reason
is that since kis a constant that does not increase with the dimension d, the number of exploration
is of order O(√
T) instead of the desired O(/radicalbig
dTlog(T)). In other words, the (original) MLE-Cycle
algorithm under-explores. Therefore, a simple ﬁx is to make kincrease with the dimension d. In
particular, in the modiﬁed MLE-Cycle algorithm, we set the l ength of price experiments for the cth
39
cycle as
k=/radicalbig
dlog(2c).
By simple algebra, for all suﬃciently large T,the number of price experiments conducted by the
modiﬁed MLE-Cycle till the Tth round is of order O(/radicalbig
dTlog(T)). Using similar arguments as the
ones in our proof of Theorem 2.2, we can show that, under similar conditions as the ones used i n
Theorem 2.3, the modiﬁed MLE-Cycle can achieve the near-optimal regret of orderO(/radicalbig
dTlog(T)).
Algorithm S.4 The MLE-Cycle algorithm for dynamic pricing
Input: Price interval [ l,u], exploration length kand Ψ = ∅.
forcyclesc= 1,2,···do
a. (Exploration): For the ﬁrst krounds, randomly choose pt∈[l,u], and record yt
andxt= (z⊤
t,−ptz⊤
t)⊤into the price experiment set Ψ.
c. (Estimation): Obtain MLE /hatwideθbased on observations in Ψ.
d. (Exploitation): For the next crounds, oﬀer the greedy price at p∗(/hatwideθ,zt) based on/hatwideθ.
end for
S.5.5 The Semi-Myopic algorithm and a simple modiﬁcation
The Semi-Myopic type algorithm is a class of pricing policie s modiﬁed from the greedy pricing policy
and is proposed in Keskin and Zeevi (2014) for dynamic pricing under an unknown linear demand
model with no covariates (i.e. context-free). Variants of S emi-Myopic algorithms have also been
proposed in the literature, such as Controlled Variance in Den Boer and Zwart (2014) for context-free
dynamic pricing and Random Shock Design in Simchi-Levi and Wang (2024) for contextual dynamic
pricing (and its statistical inference problem).
These works show Semi-Myopic can provide a regret of order /tildewideO(√
T), without investigating the
impact of dimension d(i.e. treating the dimension das ﬁxed). In fact, one can easily show that
ford-dimensional contextual dynamic pricing, these algorithm s achieve a regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T).
We summarize a variant of Semi-Myopic in Algorithm S.5, which is based on the CILS algorithm in
Keskin and Zeevi (2014) and the random shock design in Simchi-Levi and Wang (2024).
The key idea of the Semi-Myopic algorithm is to inject a caref ully calibrated price deviation into
theprice given by the greedy algorithm (which is myopic), he ncethe nameSemi-Myopic. Inparticular,
40
at each round t, given context zt, the ﬁrm oﬀers a price at p∗(/hatwideθ,zt)+δt, wherep∗(/hatwideθ,zt) is the greedy
price based on the estimated parameter /hatwideθandδt=κt−1/4·Btis the injected price deviation with
Btbeing a Rademacher random variable. Importantly, one can sh ow that thanks to the injected
price deviation, for all suﬃciently large t,the minimum eigenvalue of the design matrix based on
observations in rounds {1,2,···,t}is guaranteed to be of order Ω(√
tλz), which is the key component
for achieving a sublinear regret.
In the above mentioned literature, κis set as an absolute constant (typically 1) and Semi-Myopic
gives a regret of order /tildewideO(d√
T), which is sub-optimal - supported by Theorem 4.1. The intuitive
reason is that since κis a constant that does not increase with the dimension d, making the minimum
eigenvalue of the design matrix take the order Ω(√
tλz) instead of the desired Ω(√
dtλz). In other
words, the (original) Semi-Myopic algorithm under-explor es and injects less price deviation than the
optimal level. Therefore, a simple ﬁx is to make κincrease with the dimension d. In particular, in
the modiﬁed Semi-Myopic algorithm, we instead set the price deviation for round tasκ=d1/4·t−1/4.
Using similar arguments as the ones in our proof of Theorem 2.2, we can show that, under similar
conditions as the ones used in Theorem 2.3, the modiﬁed Semi-Myopic can achieve the near-optimal
regret of/tildewideO(√
dT).
Computational consideration : Note that due to its design, Semi-Myopic require the comput a-
tion ofO(T) many MLEs, which is computationally much more intensive th an MLE-Cycle and ETC.
Therefore, in our implementation, we only run step (b) and (d ) of Semi-Myopic every 5 rounds, which
reduces its computational cost while still maintaining its theoretical guarantees.
Algorithm S.5 The Semi-Myopic algorithm for dynamic pricing
Input: Price interval [ l,u], exploration parameter κand Ψ = ∅.
fort= 1,2,···do
a. (Price deviation): Set δt=κt−1/4·Bt, whereBtis a Rademacher random variable.
b. (Estimation): Obtain MLE /hatwideθbased on observations.
c. (Semi-Exploitation): Oﬀer price ptatp∗(/hatwideθ,zt)+δt, wherep∗(/hatwideθ,zt) is the greedy price
based on the MLE.
d. (Record data): Record ytandxt= (z⊤
t,−ptz⊤
t)⊤into the set Ψ.
end for
41

S.6.1 LDP guarantees
LemmaS.6.1is directly from Section I.2 in Duchi et al. (2018) and we omit its proof. It ensures the
LDP guarantees of Algorithm 4.
Lemma S.6.1. Let W(g) be the output of L2-ball(g,Cg,ǫ). Then, W( g) is anǫ-LDP view of g, and
E[W(g)|g] =gand/⌊ard⌊lW(g)/⌊ard⌊l=Cgrǫ,d.
Proposition S.6.1. The LDP-ETC policy is ǫ-LDP.

By deﬁnition, for all st,s′

The following lemma gives a uniform concentration inequali ty for martingale diﬀerence sequences
thatareboundedorsatisfycertainmomentconditions(e.g. sub-Gaussian), whichisusefulforbounding
the variance component of the SGD estimator in Theorem 3.1(ǫ-LDP), Lemma S.6.4(mixed privacy
constraint) and Lemma S.6.5(approximate LDP). It is a generalization of Lemma 3 in Rakhlin et al.
2011, which only covers the case of bounded random variables.

S.6.4 Proof of Theorem 3.3
LemmaS.6.4gives a high-probability upper bound of the estimation erro r for the two-stage SGD
estimator in the exploration phase of ETC-LDP-Mixed, servi ng as the basis for bounding the regret
of ETC-LDP-Mixed. For convenience of presentation, we ﬁrst recap the two-stage SGD estimator in
ETC-LDP-Mixed with slightly diﬀerent notations.
Suppose we have τ1private sample points under the ǫ-LDP constraint and τ2non-private sample
points. The two-stage SGD combines a private SGD and a non-pr ivate SGD to estimate the model
parameterθ∗. In particular, it ﬁrst runs a private SGD on the τ1private data as in Theorem 3.1,
giving the output /hatwideθτ1. It then runs a non-private SGD with /hatwideθτ1as the initial point and step size
η∗
τ1+t=ζ−1
l(τ1ǫ2/d+t)−1,fort= 1,2,···,τ2, (S.33)
on theτ2non-private data, which gives the ﬁnal estimator /hatwideθτ1+τ2.
We remark that for the non-private SGD, we use the true gradie nt instead of truncating the
gradient to a bounded vector. In other words, the gradient ca n be potentially unbounded. Therefore,
the technical argument for Lemma S.6.4needs to be substantially modiﬁed compared to that in
Theorem 3.1. In particular, we rely on the newly developed Lemma S.6.2and Lemma S.6.3to control
the unbounded gradient. Technically, we extend the uniform concentration inequality for martingale
diﬀerence sequences in Rakhlin et al. (2011) from bounded ran dom variables to random variables with
certain moment conditions (e.g. sub-Gaussian), which is of independent interest.

S.6.5 Dynamic pricing under approximate LDP
Deﬁnition S.6.1deﬁnes the notion of approximate LDP for dynamic pricing, wh ich is modiﬁed from
the notion of (pure) LDP for dynamic pricing in Deﬁnition 3.1of the main text.
Deﬁnition S.6.1 ((ǫ,∆)-LDP for dynamic pricing) .For anyǫ >0 and ∆∈(0,1], a pricing policy
π={Qt,At}T
t=1satisﬁes (ǫ,∆)-LDP if for any t∈[T],w<t, andst,s′
t, it holds that for any measurable
setW,Qt(W|st,w<t)≤eǫQt(W|s′
t,w<t)+∆.
It is clear from the deﬁnition that ( ǫ,∆)-LDP is a relaxed privacy constraint than ǫ-LDP, as
it allows a ∆∈(0,1] probability of privacy leakage. In the following, we prop ose an ETC-LDP-
Approx algorithm in Algorithm S.6and further establish its theoretical guarantees in Lemma S.6.5
and Theorem S.6.1. ETC-LDP-Approx in Algorithm S.6follows the same structure as ETC-LDP in
Algorithm 3. The only diﬀerence lies in their privacy mechanism. In parti cular, to achieve ( ǫ,∆)-LDP,
we employ the widely used Gaussian mechanism in ETC-LDP-App rox (see step a2) to privatize the
truncated gradient g[C]
tintowt, instead of the L2-ball mechanism in ETC-LDP.

S.6.5.1 Numerical experiments
In this section, we further conduct numerical experiments t o examine the ﬁnite sample performance
of ETC-LDP-Approx under ( ǫ,∆)-LDP and compare it with ETC-LDP.
In particular, we use the exact same simulation setting in Se ction5.2.3of the main text. For
56
ETC-LDP-Approx, we ﬁx ǫ= 1 and vary the privacy leakage probability ∆ ∈{10−2,10−3}. The
result under ǫ∈{2,4}is similar and thus omitted to conserve space.
FigureS.12(left) reports the mean regret RT,d,ǫof ETC-LDP-Approx with ∆ ∈{10−2,10−3}at
diﬀerent (T,d) andǫ= 1 under (S1). As can be seen, given a ﬁxed d, the regret scales sublinearly with
respect toT, and given a ﬁxed horizon T, the regret grows with the dimension d.Moreover, a smaller
privacy leakage probability ∆ leads to a higher regret, whic h is intuitive and conﬁrms the theoretical
results in Theorem S.6.1.
For illustration, Figure S.12(middle) further gives the boxplot of {R(i)

ǫ= 1 with a ﬁxed d= 6.As can be seen, the regret given by the two algorithms are simi lar with
ETC-LDP giving slightly lower regret (and smaller variance ). This conﬁrms our theoretical results
that the two algorithms share the same order of regret upper b ound up to a O(/radicalbig
log(1.25/∆) term.

S.7 Technical details in Section 4
S.7.1 Roadmap of the proof
In this subsection, we ﬁrst provide a roadmap of the technica l proof for the lower bound construction
under both non-private and private dynamic pricing, where w e point out key steps and key lemmas
used in the proof and discuss their intuition. There are in to tal 4 steps (Step 0 - Step 3).
Step 0. Lower bound construction. [Section S.7.2]
Weﬁrstconstructasetof2drevenuefunctions, indexedbythehyperparameter v= (v1,···,vd)⊤∈
{0,1}dsuch that distinguishing vi= 0 fromvi= 1 for each coordinate can only depend on the
information from the ith covariate. Speciﬁcally, let zt= (I(Mt= 1),···,I(Mt=d))⊤∈{0,1}d, where
Mtis a multinomial distribution on {1,···,d}with equal marginal probability. Consider the following
demand model indexed by the hyper parameter v= (v1,···,vd)⊤∈{0,1}d, such that
yt=Ev[yt|zt,pt]+εtandEv[yt|zt,pt] :=λv(zt,pt) = 2−pt+∆d/summationdisplay
i=1vi(1−pt)I(Mt=i),
where{εt}T
t=1is a sequence of independent N(0,1) random variables, and ∆ ∈[0,1/2] quantiﬁes the
diﬃculty of the problem, and will be chosen diﬀerently for the non-private and private cases. Note
by our construction, the eﬀective information for viis contained through the ith element of zt, i.e.
I(Mt=i) only.
ForMt=i, an important property of our construction is the following connection of the instant
60
regret and the policy induced price:

Step 1. Reduction to classiﬁcation problem. [Lemma S.7.1for the non-private case, and
LemmaS.7.4for the private case.]
[The non-private case, Lemma S.7.1] We ﬁrst construct a classiﬁer using data st={zt,pt,yt}
such thatψ: (s1,···,sT)→{ˆvi}d
i=1∈{0,1}d. In Lemma S.7.1, we show, with high probability, for
any non-anticipating policy π,
T/summationdisplay

144T∆2d−1d/summationdisplay

This implies that if there is a low-regret pricing policy, th en there is a classiﬁer ψthat achieves
small classiﬁcation errors. Therefore, this motivates us t o use information-theoretical tools (Assouad’s
Lemma in particular) to show a lower bound on the classiﬁcati on error.
[The private case, Lemma S.7.4]UndertheLDPconstraint, wecannotdirectly usetheclassi ﬁer de-
ﬁned in the non-private setting as it utilizes non-privatiz ed contextual information. Instead, we adopt
the construction in Lemma 3 of Chen et al. (2022a) and augment the ǫ-LDP policy πto construct a 2 ǫ-
LDP policy π′. In particular, we have to additionally privatize the infor mation used for classiﬁcation,
which necessitates elevating the regret analysis from an ǫ-LDP policy to a 2 ǫ-LDP policy. To address
this, we privatize the information used in the classiﬁcatio n process by introducing Laplacian noise into
the response vector of the classiﬁcation. Lemma S.7.4then shows that, with high probability, for any
non-anticipating ǫ-LDP policy π, there exists a 2 ǫ-LDP classiﬁer ψ: (w1,···,wT)→ˆv∈{0,1}d, such
that (S.50) still holds.

[The private case, Lemma S.7.8] This is the key component of our lower bound construction
to derive the Ω( d√
