instead of using the KL divergence as in the non-private case , we resort to the sharper Hellinger
distance to further bound TV. In fact, for two arbitrary dist ributionsP1andP2, we have

The proof of Lemma S.7.8is a key component and novelty of our lower bound constructio n and we
give a more detailed discussion of this step in Step 2A below, where we discuss the technical challenge
and important modiﬁcations we made to adapt the information contraction bound in Acharya et al.
(2024) (designed for i.i.d. data) to the setting of private dynami c pricing (where data is adaptive).
We remark that our analysis in Lemma S.7.8is new and ﬁrst time seen in the dynamic pricing
literature. In fact, existing lower bound construction in c ontextual bandit ( Han et al. ,2021) and con-
textual (non-parametric) dynamic pricing ( Chen et al. ,2022a) under LDP constraints mainly rely on
the well-known private Le Cam Lemma (Theorem 1 therein) and p rivate Assouad’s Lemma (Proposi-
tion 3 therein) developed in Duchi et al. (2018), combined with KL divergence bounds. However, we
ﬁnd that this strategy (i.e. technical tools in Duchi et al. (2018) + KL divergence) can only derive a
Ω(√
dT/ǫ) lower bound, which is not sharp in dimension d.Instead, our proof strategy is to leverage
theHellinger distanceand modifytheinformation contract ion boundin Acharya et al. (2024) toderive
the sharp Ω( d√
T/ǫ) bound.
Step 3. Finish the proof . Therefore, to summarize, for both the non-private and priv ate cases,
we eﬀectively create a dilemma for the regret of any non-antic ipating policy. In particular, for small
values of ∆, we can always identify a setting where the associ ated classiﬁcation error is large due
to the Assouad’s Lemma. Conversely, for large values of ∆, as is implied by ( S.49), the cumulative
63
regret also becomes large. The result then follows by carefu lly balancing two scenarios and choosing
an appropriate value of ∆.
Step 2A. Key modiﬁcation of Acharya et al. (2024) for LDP dynamic pricing [Step II in
the proof Lemma S.7.8and Lemma S.7.9.]
We ﬁrst introduce the information contraction boundresult inAcharya et al. (2024), which applies
to statistical estimation problems under ǫ-LDP but for i.i.d. data only. We then discuss the key non-
trivial modiﬁcations we made to tailor for dynamic pricing u nderǫ-LDP, where the data is no longer
i.i.d. but instead can be adaptively collected by anynon-anticipating pricing policy π.

The proof of Corollary 1 uses similar idea in our Step 2 above b y bounding the total variation distance

Key modiﬁcations made totailor for dynamic pricing under ǫ-LDP.Theresult Acharya et al.
(2024) only applies to i.i.d. data and thus is not directly applica ble to dynamic pricing under ǫ-LDP,
where the data{st= (yt,zt,pt)}can be adaptively generated by any non-anticipating pricin g policy
π. In particular, note that the price ptgiven byπat any time point tis a function of the current
covariateztand all historical private observations w<t={w1,···,wt−1}(see (9) and (10) in the
main text), which clearly makes {st}non-i.i.d. with complex inter-dependence. Therefore, non -trivial
modiﬁcation is required to adapt the technical tools in Acharya et al. (2024) to our setting of dynamic
pricing under ǫ-LDP. Our main technical contributions are as follows.
First, to address the complex inter-dependence of the raw da ta{st}, we develop a novel decompo-
sition argument and show that instead of analyzing the (intr actable) marginal distribution of stdue
to adaptivity of π, we can analyze the conditional distribution of stgiven past private observations
w<t, denoted by dPπ
v,t(st|w<t)/dst. Importantly, leveraging the two-part structure of the LDP pricing
policyπ, as described in ( 9) and (10) of the main text, we show that dPπ
v,t(st|w<t)/dstis in fact
tractable. Denote ϕv,i(st|w<t) as the score function deﬁned as in Acharya-Assumption 1 abo ve but for
dPπ
v,t(st|w<t)/dst, we show in Lemma S.7.9thatϕv,i(st|w<t) still exhibits nice structures. In particu-
lar,ϕv,i(st|w<t) is a martingale diﬀerence and the orthogonality condition i n Acharya-Assumption 2
65
above still holds for ϕv,i(st|w<t). This is indeed intuitive as by our lower bound constructio n in Step
0, the instant information gain at time tstems solely from the current active dimension i, with no
contribution from other inactive dimensions.
Second, Acharya-Assumption 2 imposes a constant bound αon the variation of the score function
ϕv,i, which is suﬃcient for lower bound construction for statist ical estimation. However, this is not
suﬃcient for our purposes as we need to establish a bound on th e TV that is a function of the regret of
πas is in (S.51). We address this by deriving a new upper bound on the varianc e ofϕv,i. In particular,
in the proof of Lemma S.7.9, we show its variance can be further upper bounded by a quadra tic term
in both the policy-induced price and the optimal price, whic h subsequently leads to the derivation of
the instant regret due to ( S.49).
S.7.2 Lower bound construction
At a high level, we construct a set of 2drevenue functions, indexed by the hyper parameter v=
(v1,···,vd)⊤∈{0,1}dsuch that distinguishing vi= 0 fromvi= 1 for each coordinate can only depend
on the information from the ith covariate. This implies that learning the parameters ass ociated with
theith covariate can only make use of the data sample of eﬀective sa mple size of order O(T/d). In
addition, by carefully crafting the model parameters, the r evenue functions in the set are diﬃcult to
distinguish. Therefore, it is very costly to diﬀerentiate th ese functions, which will inevitably incur
large regret.

classiﬁcation error. In particular, it shows that the regre t of anyǫ-LDP policy πcan be lower bounded
by the classiﬁcation error of an associated 2 ǫ-LDP classiﬁer.

Similar to Lemma S.7.2, we need to lower bound the classiﬁcation error under LDP by t he total
variation distance between distributions due to Assouad’s Lemma. However, it turns out that the
proof technique in Lemma S.7.2using KL divergence to bound total variation distance will y ield a
less sharp result in terms of the dimension d. Instead, we establish a sharp bound using the Hellinger
distance. In fact, for two arbitrary distributions P1andP2, we have
{TV(P1,P2)}2≤2H2(P1,P2)≤DKL(P1,P2),
where H2(P1,P2) =1

supportX.The above inequality implies that using the Hellinger dista nce allows for more delicate
analysis than the KL divergence.

Lemma S.7.5. Let two mixture distributions FandGbe
F=aF0+(1−a)F1andG=aG0+(1−a)G1,
wherea∈(0,1), andF0,F1,G0,G1are distributions with common support X. Then,
H2(F,G)≤aH2(F0,G0)+(1−a)H2(F1,G1).
Proof.Letf0,f1,g0,g1be associated probability density/mass functions of F0,F1,G0,G1. Similarly
we deﬁnefandgforFandG, respectively. Note H2(F,G) = 1−/integraltext

This implies that H2(F,G) is convex in a.The result follows.
